金融数据的三个边际改善 
——2026 年 9 月金融数据点评

事件：9 月新增社融 3.55 万亿，同比多增 192 亿元，存量社融
增速 7.1%，较上月下降 0.1 个百分点；金融机构新增 人民币贷款
1.08 万亿，同比少增2100 亿元，金融机构各项人民币贷款余额增速
4.8%，较上月下降 0.1 个百分点；M1 同比增长 3.2%，较上月下降
0.9 个百分点，M2 同比增长7.2%，较上月下降 0.3 个百分点。 
政府债券 发行提速 ，支撑 9 月社融同比多增。 9 月新增社融
3.55 万亿，同比多增 192 亿元，社融存量增速为 7.1%，较上月下降
0.1 个百分点。从新增社融结构来看，9 月新增政府债券融资 1.53
万亿，高于季节性 水平（2021-2025 年同期均值，下同 ），同比多
增 3438 亿元，是本月社融同比多增的重要支撑，表明财政发力节奏
加快、为四季度基建投资回升提供资金基础；企业债券融资减少
425 亿元、股票融资新增658 亿元，合计同比少增402 亿元，企业直
接融资走弱，可能与近期股市阶段性调整有关。间接融资方面， 9
月新增人民币贷款1.27 万亿，显著低于季节性水平（2.09 万亿），
同比少增 3342 亿元；人民币持续升值下，新增外币贷款 349 亿元，
同比多增478 亿元；新增三项非标2477 亿元，同比少增1102 亿元，
但其中，新型政策性金融工具投放拉动下，9 月新增委托贷款802 亿
元，同比多增 519 亿元。从前三季度整体情况来看，新增社融规模
基本持平于季节性水平，但内部结构发生较大变化，人民币贷款少
增，政府债券、企业直接融资多增，直接间接融资分化加剧。 今年
前三季度新增社融 27.46 万亿，基本持平于季节性水平（27.52 万
亿）。但从结构来看，新增人民币贷款 11.5 万亿，新增政府债券
10.3 万亿，新增企业债券和股票融资合计1.23 万亿，分别较季节性
水平减少 5.33 万亿、增加 3.31 万亿和增加 0.84 万亿。直接、间接
融资一增一减下，9 月末直接融资在新增社融中占比达 49.48%，较
去年同期上升 5.1 个百分点，而新增人民币贷款占比则较去年同期
下降6.45 个百分点至41.88%。 
金融数据点评 2026 年10 月15 日  2026 年第57 期

9 月金融数据点评： 
金融数据的三个边际改善 
居民信贷零新增，企业新增信贷低于季节性水平。9 月金融机构新增人民币贷款 1.08 万
亿，同比少增 2100 亿元，连续 13 个月同比少增。从结构来看，居民部门新增短期、中长期
信贷一负一正。9 月居民部门新增人民币贷款 0 亿元，同比少增 3890 亿元，其中，短期贷款
减少 900 亿元，同比少增 2321 亿元，已连续三个月同比少增，一是居民收入预期疲弱，消费
意愿不强；二是补贴政策退坡后汽车零售减少，根据乘联会数据， 9 月全国乘用车市场零售
161.8 万辆，同比下降 28.0%，9 月“旺季不旺”，或导致汽车消费贷减少；三是经营贷利率
上调、银行不再硬性考核普惠小微贷款增速、经营贷不良率攀升，共同导致经营贷规模缩减，
9 月短期经营贷同比少增约1500 亿元。9 月居民部门新增中长期贷款874 亿元，同比少增1626
亿元，与地产销售数据表现一致，9 月 30 大中城市商品房成交面积同比下降 10.75%，“金九”
成色不佳。企业新增信贷同比由负转正。9 月企业部门新增人民币贷款 1.29 万亿，同比多增
700 亿元，结束了连续三个月的同比少增。这主要源于票据融资基数较低，9 月票据融资规模
减少 500 亿元，低基数下同比少减 3526 亿元；从短期、中长期贷款来看，企业 9 月新增短期、
中长期贷款分别为 4600 亿元和 8900 亿元，分别同比少增 2500 亿元和 200 亿元，但中长期贷
款少增幅度显著收缩。从前三季度整体情况来看，1-9 月新增人民币贷款11.52 万亿，同比少
增 3.23 万亿，其中，居民部门同比少增 2.13 万亿，企业部门同比少增 0.89 万亿。实体信贷
需求仍然偏弱，但降幅已开始收窄。 从居民部门来看，三季度居民部门中长期贷款同比少增
2750 亿元，少增幅度较一、二季度均显著收窄。从企业部门来看，三季度企业新增贷款中，
中长期贷款占比开始上升，9 月该占比已上升至52.03%。

9 月金融数据点评： 
金融数据的三个边际改善 
M1、M2 增速均边际下行，M2-M1 剪刀差走扩。9 月 M1 同比增长 3.2%，较上月下降 0.9
个百分点，或主要源于去年同期基数较高。去年9 月M1 新增1.92 万亿，显著高于2021-2025
年同期均值（4339.6 亿元），而本月 M1 新增 9822 亿元，本身并不低。从各部门来看，9 月
住户活期存款余额较上月上升、非银存款余额较上月下降，资本市场收益率下降下居民风险
偏好下降，“存款搬家”暂缓；非银企业活期存款余额结束了连续两个月环比下降的趋势，
较上月增长5556 亿元，表明9 月进入生产旺季后，企业开支需求增加，但持续性仍待观察；
财政存款余额较上月下降 4360 亿元，财政支出力度加大。9 月 M2 同比增长 7.2%，较上月下
降0.3 个百分点，主要源于贷款增速下降，阻碍货币派生。

9 月金融数据点评： 
金融数据的三个边际改善 
9 月人民币汇率整体走强，月中突破 6.70 关键点位。9 月人民币在岸汇率在 6.6955 至
6.7219 间波动，人民币离岸汇率在6.6927 至6.7223 间波动，在岸、离岸人民币报价无显著
背离趋势。月初至 21 日，欧、美、日等主要经济体集体上调基准利率，但人民币仍呈现出
震荡上行的独立行情，并于 21 日突破了 6.70 的关键点位，在岸、离岸人民币汇率分别达到
6.6955 和6.6927，创2023 年2 月以来新高。9 月下旬美元兑人民币汇率掉头回升至6.70 以
上，30 日，在岸、离岸人民币汇率分别为6.7053 和6.7088。19 日，中国人民银行货币政策
委员会召开 2026 年第三季度例会，汇率方面新增强调“坚持市场在汇率形成中的决定性 作
用” ，以及“防范市场‘羊群效应’和非理性预期的自我强化” ，释放稳汇率基调。从前三季
度尤其是 9 月人民币汇率走势来看，结汇需求而非中美利差已成为影响人民币汇率定价的主
要因素。1-9 月，美元指数整体上涨 3%，中美利差（美国 10 年期国债收益率-中国 10 年期
国债收益率）较年初上升约 128 个基点至 3.6%，但人民币汇率却上涨了约 4%，表明中美利
差倒挂不再是影响人民币汇率的主要因素，人民币汇率上行的主要动力在于结汇需求旺盛。

数据来源：Wind,中诚信国际

9 月金融数据点评： 
金融数据的三个边际改善 
1-9 月，我国出口同比增长21.3%，贸易顺差为9381 亿美元，催生了大量结汇需求，1-8 月，
我国银行代客结汇金额为13.97 万亿元、同比增长26.3%，代客结售汇差额为2.74 万亿元、
较去年同期增长了 7.6 倍。此外，人民币升值预期也提升了结汇需求，反过来又推动人民币
汇率进一步上行，形成了人民币汇率的自我强化，这也是央行强调“防范市场‘羊群效应’
和非理性预期的自我强化”的重要原因。

9 月金融数据点评： 
金融数据的三个边际改善 
整体来看，9 月金融数据呈现出一定的边际改善。 一是财政发力显著，9 月新增政府债
券融资规模同比、环比上升，财政存款余额环比下降；二是企业融资表现有所改善，9 月委
托贷款同比多增，新型政策性金融工具发力见效，企业新增贷款同比多增，且新增企业贷款
中中长期贷款占比边际上升，同时企业部门活期存款余额由降转增，均表明企业开支和融资
意愿增强；三是居民新增中长期贷款转正，且三季度新增中长期贷款同比降幅较一、二季度
明显收窄。但需要注意的是，在内需不足和预期偏弱的背景下，实体部门融资需求边际改善
的持续性仍需政策呵护。展望后续，四季度实体部门融资需求有望温和改善，社融增速或小
幅回升。其一，9 月底 PSL 降息扩围、增加科创与技术改造再贷款与支农支小再贷款额度、
居民购房贷款贴息等一系列政策效果有望在四季度显现；其二，四季度地方债发行有望提速，
1-9 月，地方政府新增一般债、地方债发行进度均低于去年和过去三年同期水平，四季度仍
有较大空间；其三，财政部近日表示安排使用地方政府债务结存限额 5500 亿元，用于提高
县区一般公共预算保障能力和支持地方扩大有效投资 ，有望缓解地方财政收支矛盾并托底四
季度及明年初基建投资；其四，今年 8000 亿元新型政策性金融工具于 9 月开始投放，目前
国开新型政策性金融工具已投放资金约600 亿元，后续仍有约7000 亿规模有待使用；其五，
9.28 国常会明确加大逆周期调节力度，并推出一批务实管用的增量政策，后续根据经济运行
情况，降准、增发超长期特别国债、增加政策性金融工具额度等政策有望落地。 
汇率方面，9 月人民币汇率震荡上行，四季度人民币或将双向浮动为主、温 和升值为辅。
一方面，中国经济保持韧性，9 月制造业 PMI 指数回升至荣枯线以上，同时，中国制造业持
续升级叠加全球 AI 浪潮延续，出口仍有一定支撑，企业结汇需求仍存。但另一方面，央行
明确需防范“羊群效应” ，后续若人民币汇率升值过快，央行或将加大调控力度。此外，美

9 月金融数据点评：
金融数据的三个边际改善
国 9 月非农、通胀数据的反向指示表明今年美联储或仍有一次加息，也将对人民币汇率造成
一定扰动。整体来看，四季度人民币汇率或将保持双向浮动，若出口和结汇需求较为强劲，
尤其年末企业结汇需求抬升，人民币汇率或将小幅升值。另外，央行在近期发布的《中国人
民银行关于人民币汇率的政策立场》表明，中国无意通过人民币贬值提升出口竞争力，也不
会因为外部压力推动人民币升值，而是坚持让市场在汇率形成中发挥决定性作用，保持汇率
弹性和双向浮动，降低了汇率政策的不确定性，稳定了市场预期。

Document 067: 2026年金融街论坛学习体会：货币政策传导效率进一步提升

2026年金融街论坛学习体会

货币政策传导效率进一步提升   
主要政策利率即将换锚 。贷款利率定价基准趋向多元化。进一步出台稳市举
措。支持 AI 发展，推进 AI 应用。 
 10 月 19 日，2026 金融街论坛年会正式开幕，年会主题为“开放・合作・
包容——共筑全球金融发展新生态”。 
 主要政策利率 即将换锚。中国人民银行行长潘功胜发表题为《 关于当前
市场关注若干问题的思考 》的主题演讲。潘行长 在演讲中指出，DR001
“作为货币市场交易活跃、最具基准性的利率，能够灵敏反映短期资金
供求状况”，已成为央行货币政策调控的目标利率。 
考虑到当前央行公开市场操作的主要品种及利率期限均仍为 7 天，潘行
长指出，央行“将研究择机把央行公开市场主要操作品种的期限切换为
隔夜，公开市场隔夜回购利率将相应成为主要政策利率 ”。此次调整将
主要政策利率与目标利率 的期限对齐（均为隔夜） ，将央行日常流动性
管理精准至 单日，短端利率调控机制 得到有效优化， 有助于提升利率传
导效率。 
从我国利率走廊建设的角度看，2024 年央行创设临时隔夜正/逆回购工具，
利率为 7 天逆回购利率−20bp / +50bp，作为我国利率走廊边界。2026 年 6
月央行进一步优化利率走廊，将临时隔夜正/逆回购工具利率调整为 7 天
逆回购利率−20bp / + 20bp，但利率走廊操作基准利率仍为 7 天逆回购利
率。央行此次将公开市场主要操作品种期限切换 至隔夜，完成了基准利
率、走廊边界及目标利率期限 的对齐，有望进一步提升我国流动性管理
政策的精准度，平缓未来利率波动。 
 贷款利率定价基准 趋向多元化。潘行长在讲话中指出， 未来“财务能力
强的企业”可在 DR001 和 LPR 之间自主选择浮动利率贷款定价基准，LPR
不再是贷款 利率的单一基准，一定程度上赋予了企业、银行 在信贷活动
中定价基准的选择权。这一举措或能在 疏通央行政策传导渠道的同时，
降低实体融资成本。当前我国 LPR 报价按月公布，DR001 则为日频成交
价格，后者对银行间资金成本反映更加充分；以 DR001 为贷款利率定价
基准，或能 使贷款价格与银行间流动性 水平进一步对齐 ，在银行间流动
性宽松的情况下，推动“财务能力强的企业”融资成本下降。 
 进一步出台 稳市举措。潘行长在讲话中 提出“设立应对非银机构大额赎
回的流动性支持工具 ”，旨在进一步 建立债券市场稳定机制。此外，证
监会主席吴清也在讲话中提出 “加强风险研判和政策工具储备，巩固完
善稳市机制建设 ”，“扩大中长期资金来源、渠道和入市方式 ”，“壮
大市场稳定力量”。 
 支持 AI 发展，推进 AI 应用。潘行长指出央行要做好三方面工作：“密
切跟踪研判人工智能发展的宏观影响 ”；“做好金融支持人工智能产业
发展工作”；“稳妥推进人工智能在金融领域的应用” ； 并特别提出“完
善人工智能企业融资支持体系 ”，金融对科技创新的支持强度有望进一
步提升。在推进 AI 应用方面，吴清主席也在讲话中指出将“发布实施人
工智能在证券期货业安全合规应用的意见”，并促进 AI“在风控、投研
等重点场景安全高效应用”。 
 风险提示：海外大宗商品价格震荡；地缘关系的不确定性；政策效果不
及预期。

评级体系说明 
以报告发布日后公司股价/行业指数涨跌幅相对同期相关市场指数的涨跌幅的表现为基准： 
公司投资评级： 
买    入：预计该公司股价在未来 6-12 个月内超越基准指数 20%以上； 
增    持：预计该公司股价在未来 6-12 个月内超越基准指数 10%-20%； 
中    性：预计该公司股价在未来 6-12 个月内相对基准指数变动幅度在-10%-10%之间； 
减    持：预计该公司股价在未来 6-12 个月内相对基准指数跌幅在 10%以上； 
未有评级：因无法获取必要的资料或者其他原因，未能给出明确的投资评级。

行业投资评级： 
强于大市：预计该行业指数在未来 6-12 个月内表现强于基准指数； 
中    性：预计该行业指数在未来 6-12 个月内表现基本与基准指数持平； 
弱于大市：预计该行业指数在未来 6-12 个月内表现弱于基准指数； 
未有评级：因无法获取必要的资料或者其他原因，未能给出明确的投资评级。

沪深市场基准指数为沪深 300 指数；新三板市场基准指数为三板成指或三板做市指数；香港市场基准指数为恒生指数
或恒生中国企业指数；美股市场基准指数为纳斯达 克综合指数或标普 500 指数。

旺矽營收優於預期 ，CPO 設備出貨將帶動評價提升 ， 給Buy
的投資建議。  
投資建議 ：旺矽營收優於預期， CPO 設備出貨將帶動評價提升，
給 Buy 的投資建議。  
09/2026 營收優於群益預期 ：旺矽 09/2026 營收為 20.48 億元，
MoM-0.02% 、 YoY+82.23% ，累計營收為 150.68 億元，
YoY+58.02% 。實際月營收與群益預估的 16.50 億元，差異為
19.43% ，優於預期。  
3Q26 優於市場預期 ：市場先前預期旺矽 3Q26 營收 QoQ+0~5% ，
實際營收 QoQ+12.76% ，優於預期。  
旺矽有佈局 CPO 測試設備 ：旺矽的 Insertion 3 設備預計 4Q26
向晶圓代工 、IC 設計 、OSAT 送樣 ，1H27 有望進入量產 ；Insertion 
2 設備預計 2H26 會有較明顯的結果。  
旺矽積極擴產： 旺矽表示，目前在許多客戶端都是第一供應商，
供貨比重大多為 80% ，但之前因為產能供不應求，有外溢到第
二供應商，現在有積極擴產。  
旺矽預計 2027 年提高 PCB 的自製率： 探針卡可分為測試頭、
載板及 PCB 。旺矽表示，探針及載板均已全數自製， PCB 預計
2027 年開始量產，目標將 PCB 的自製率提升至 50% 。

益預估旺矽 2026 年 /2027 年營收為 214.11 億元 /363.85 億元，

增進社區認同。

將資源投入透過商業模式解決社會或環境問題之組織，或參與社區發展及
社區教育之公民組織、慈善公益團體及政府機構之相關活動，以促進社區發
展。

上櫃公司訂定道德行為準則參考範例，建置有效之治理架構及相關道德標
準，以健全公司治理。

6 群益投顧

  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首次評等潛在上漲空間 ≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy) 5% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <15%

中立 (Neutral)  
無法由基本面給予投資評等  
預期近期股價將處於盤整  
建議降低持股

【投資評等調整機制】  
  投資評等係「首次」給予特定個股投資評等時，其前一日收盤價相較 3 個月
目標價之潛在上漲空間計算而得。個股投資評等分為四個等級，定義如上。
爾後的投資評等係依循「首次評等」，直到停止推薦。   
「強力買進」、「買進」及「區間操作」均有 upside 目標價。差別僅在於，
首次評等時潛在報酬率不同。「中立」則無目標價。  
一旦我們給予特定個股「強力買進」 、 「買進」或「區間操作」之投資評等，
就是責任的開始，爾後將透過各式研究報告作定期性、持續性追蹤基本面及
股價變化，直到停止推薦。  
停止推薦情境：  
1.  達目標價。  
2.  雖未達原訂目標價，但檢視基本面、訊息面、籌碼面等多方訊息，研
判股價上漲空間已然有限，將適時出具降評報告。  
3.  推薦後股價不漲反跌。

【免責聲明】  
  本研究報告僅提供予特定人之客戶作為參考資料「非經同意不得轉載」 。我
們並不確保此資訊的完整性與正確性，投資人應了解，報告中有關未來預測
之陳述可能不會實現，因而不應被依賴。而且此報告並非根據特定投資目的
或依預定對象之財務狀況所撰寫出來的，因此，此研究報告的目的，既非對
投資人於買賣證券、選擇權、期貨或其他證券相關之衍生性商品提供詢價服
務，亦非作為進行交易的要約。投資人應注意到相關證券之價值及收益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI交易何时止跌？市场盯着Meta“天量发债”背后的“囚徒困境”.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)

Pages: 4

### Page 1

巴克莱认为， AI 巨头争相提前融资形成 “ 囚徒困境 ” ，集体发债将推⾼融资成本，限制信⽤利
差收窄。随着低评级债券⻛险上升、 AI 发⾏⼈信⽤分化加剧，融资供给能否被市场消化，
将成为判断 AI 交易能否企稳的关键。
摘要：
过去⼀周， AI 交易遭遇新⼀轮抛售压⼒，美国股票、债券市场同步承压。纳斯达克出现数⽉来最
差单⽇表现，美国⾼收益债⻛险溢价升⾄ 17 个⽉⾼位， CCC 级垃圾债收益率则攀升⾄ 2020 年以
来最⾼⽔平。股指表⾯波动有限，底层资产的⻛险重估却在加速。
引发市场担忧的焦点之⼀，是 Meta 在公布三季度业绩后释放的巨额融资计划。据巴克莱 10 ⽉ 23
⽇发布的研究报告， Meta 选择提前融资，部分原因是 AI ⽣态内其他企业已陆续启动或计划启动
融资，资本市场的吸纳能⼒正变得越来越有限。
这揭⽰了 AI 巨头⾯临的 “ 囚徒困境 ” ：每家公司都有抢先融资的动机，但如果巨头集体加快发
债，新增供给就可能推⾼融资成本，反过来挤压所有参与者的融资空间。与此同时，对冲基⾦

持续削减杠杆， AI 相关信⽤资产的利差分化也在扩⼤。
AI 交易何时⽌跌，因⽽不只取决于科技巨头的盈利表现，还取决于市场能否消化密集的债券供
给，以及不断上升的融资成本是否会进⼀步冲击 AI 产业链。
股指表⾯平静， AI 仓位遭集中清理
过去⼀周，指数表现掩盖了个股之间的剧烈分化。⾼盛数据显⽰，其 AI 受益股与⻛险股多空组
合较 6 ⽉⾼点下跌约 55% ，年内涨幅悉数回吐；动量策略也出现⼤幅回撤，跌⾄ 2008 年⾦融危机
以来的极端⽔平。
周四，标普 500 指数下跌 1.2% ，仍有 335 只成份股上涨；等权重标普 500 指数上涨 0.7% ，市值加
权的 SPY 却收跌。⾼盛数据显⽰，对冲基⾦在此前⼋个交易⽇中有七天进⾏去杠杆操作，周四
的抛售完全由多头平仓驱动。
这表明，近期调整不仅是对 AI 估值的重新审视，也涉及拥挤仓位的集中出清。但仓位调整能否
带来持续反弹，仍取决于信⽤市场压⼒是否缓解。

### Page 2

信⽤⻛险向低评级借款⼈集中
据彭博，美国⾼收益债平均⻛险溢价升⾄ 17 个⽉⾼位，⼀级市场出现年内最⼤规模的撤单潮；
CCC 级垃圾债收益率升⾄ 15.85% ，创 2020 年以来新⾼，与 BB 级债券之间的利差也逼近 2016 年
和 2020 年的峰值。
杠杆贷款市场同样承压。价格低于⾯值 60 美分的深度困境贷款规模升⾄ 2020 年 3 ⽉以来最⾼⽔
平。摩根⼤通数据显⽰，过去 12 个⽉，价格在⾯值 80 美分及以下的贷款规模激增近 90% 。
值得注意的是，CCC 级债券利差⼤幅⾛阔之际，整体信⽤市场并未同步恶化，说明⻛险正集中
于偿债能⼒较弱的发⾏⼈。DoubleLine Capital 企业信⽤总监 Robert Cohen 指出，部分 CCC 级
借款⼈可能⾛向债务重组，但这并不意味着整个信⽤周期已经逆转。
⾼盛数据显⽰，从投资级、⾼收益信⽤衍⽣品到低评级现⾦债券，多个评级区间的利差均触及
本轮周期⾼位。 CreditSights 新加坡亚太公司信⽤主管 R. Lakshmanan 认为，多重全球⻛险接连

冲击市场后，信⽤⻛险重新定价正逐渐形成持续趋势。
Meta 发债揭⽰ AI 融资 “ 囚徒困境 ”
Meta 的融资计划将 AI 资本开⽀与信⽤市场供给之间的⽭盾推到了台前。公司将 2026 年资本开⽀
预期收窄⾄ 1350 亿⾄ 1450 亿美元，预计第四季度⽀出达 490 亿⾄ 590 亿美元；分红后的⾃由现⾦
流转负，管理层还预计从今年秋季开始融资数千亿美元。
巴克莱在报告中指出，超⼤规模科技公司的营收增⻓正在兑现，但资本开⽀仍持续攀升，融资
问题已从 “ 能否融到资 ” 转向 “ 何时融、融多少、如何融 ” 。
Meta 在申报⽂件中表⽰，鉴于 AI ⽣态内其他企业已启动或计划启动融资，资本市场的吸纳能⼒
正变得越来越有限，因此提前融资具有现实必要性。
但当所有公司都选择抢先融资，个体的理性决策就可能转化为整体市场的压⼒：新增债券供给
集中涌⼊市场，推⾼发⾏成本，并限制信⽤利差收窄。为避免过度举债损害信⽤评级，企业也
开始考虑股权融资、可转债、资产⽀持证券（ ABS ）及表外融资等渠道。

巴克莱认为， AI 相关融资供给将在短期内继续限制信⽤利差收窄空间。

2 条评论
巴克莱估算， AI 相关信⽤资产规模约为 6800 亿美元，涵盖超⼤规模科技公司、半导体、数据中
⼼及硬件企业，占投资级债券指数市值约 7.5% ，却贡献约 13% 的指数久期⻛险敞⼝（ DTS ）。
整体⽽⾔， AI 信⽤资产相较投资级指数的利差溢价约为 35 个基点，期限超过 10 年的债券溢价更
达到约 55 个基点。与此同时， AI 发⾏⼈之间的利差离散度过去⼀年从 24 个基点升⾄ 65 个基点；
即使剔除甲⻣⽂和数据中⼼板块，该指标仍上升约 60% ，达到 37 个基点。
⾃ 9 ⽉中旬以来，甲⻣⽂、 Meta 、 SpaceX 及多家数据中⼼运营商的信⽤利差扩张幅度明显超过
指数；微软、⾕歌和亚⻢逊的短期限债券则相对抗压。市场开始根据发⾏⼈的融资需求、资产

负债表和⻓期⻛险进⾏区分，⽽⾮将 AI 视为统⼀的信⽤主题。
收益率曲线也反映出⻓端压⼒。巴克莱数据显⽰，过去⼀年，超⼤规模科技公司的平均 2 年期⾄
5 年期、 5 年⾄ 10 年期及 10 年⾄ 30 年期收益率曲线分别陡峭化 137% 、 140% 和 68% 。其中， Meta
的 10 年⾄ 30 年期曲线利差达到 68 个基点，在同类发⾏⼈中最为陡峭。
⻓端曲线⾛陡，反映投资者对⻓期债务要求的⻛险补偿上升。随着 AI 企业持续扩⼤资本开⽀，市
场不仅要判断投资能否转化为收⼊，还要评估融资规模、现⾦流改善时间及新增债券对融资成
本的影响。
因此， AI 交易能否⽌跌，关键在于巨额融资能否被市场消化、信⽤利差能否企稳，以及 AI 企业
之间的⻛险分化是否继续扩⼤。在这些压⼒缓解之前，阶段性反弹仍可能受到融资成本上升的
制约。

Portfolio Considerations
We view recent volatility as a rolling
correction rather than the end of
the cycle. We favor diversified Equity

exposure, with potential opportunities
extending beyond Growth to Value,
Small- and Mid-caps, Non-U.S. Equities
(especially Emerging Markets), dividend-
focused and thematic strategies.
We favor adjusting portfolios toward
strategic durations given attractive
elevated nominal and real yields, and see
value in high-quality bonds, particularly
longer-dated municipal bonds.
Consider broader portfolio
diversification with potential
opportunities across industrial metals,

energy, Real/Tangible Assets, Hedge
Funds, private markets and global
strategies to capture themes from AI,
reindustrialization, infrastructure growth
and market dispersion.
All data, projections and opinions are as of the date of this report and subject to change.
I
N THIS  ISSUE
Macro Strategy—The  Yen, 10-Year Yield and Iran: Bessent’s Three-Front War and
the Stakes for
Investors: Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury secretary, but
these days he looks more like America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches

from T
okyo, where he is trying to engineer a stronger Japan yen, to the U.S. Treasury
market, where he is battling to keep the 10-year yield from breaking  decisively above
5%, and to T
ehran, where he has become the point man in the escalating U.S. campaign
to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Ho
w Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A decisive victory on
all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets is next to impossible,

while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best laid plans. That said, however,
the U.S. Secretary of the Treasury has signaled that he is ready to take a very  proactive,
pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less encouraging news stems
from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the Treasury secretary.
Market View—Beyond AI’s Resource Footprint, Consider its “Handprint”: Behind
ever
y Artificial Intelligence (AI)-generated response is a vast physical network of data

centers, power plants, transmission lines, cooling systems, semiconductor facilities
and critical-mineral supply chains. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power,
water, advanced hardware and raw materials could strain grids, raise emissions and
intensify pressure
on local resources. Yet the sustainability conversation should extend
beyon
d AI’s footprint to consider whether its environmental benefits can outweigh its
resource demands.
Thought of the Week—The Beauty of September: Call it an eventful first 13 trading

days of September: Brent crude re-eclipsed $100 per barrel, diesel prices surpassed
$6
per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield breached 5%, and
the Federal Reserve (Fed) joined the European Central Bank (ECB) and the Bank of Japan
(BoJ) in hiking rates for the
first time since July 2023. Through it all, the S&P 500 has
shed 1.4% since the start of the month. It’s not unexpected that September would be
choppy for U.S. Equities, especially in a midterm election year. The good news is that

this has historically been an attractive entry point. Since 1950, the S&P 500 has never
been
negative in the six or twelve months post-midterm election, boasting average
returns of 13.8% and 15.8%, respectively. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election. A correction amid a strong fundamental economic backdrop
would be a
potential buying opportunity in our view.

### Page 2

2 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
The Yen, 10-Year Yield and Iran:  Bessent’s Three-Front War and the Stakes for Investors
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury Secretary, but these days he looks more like
America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches from Tokyo, where he is trying
to engineer a stronger Japan yen; to the U.S. Treasury market, where he is battling to keep

the 10-year yield from breaking decisively above 5%; and Tehran, where he has become the
point man in the escalating U.S. campaign to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Make no mistake about it: Secretary Bessent and the Treasury Department are increasingly
an instrument of American economic statecraft, charged not simply with financing the
government and overseeing the financial system, but with managing markets, currencies
and geopolitical adversaries simultaneously. Welcome to the new age of financial warfare.

Front One: Defending the Yen. The first front unfolded over the summer when Secretary
Bessent and the BoJ stealthily intervened in support of the yen, which had fallen to a near
40-year low against the dollar by late July. To arrest the yen’s decline, the U.S. Treasury sold
euros instead of dollars in support of the yen, while pushing the BoJ to make use of the
Fed’s emergency repo facilities. In further support of the yen, the BoJ did what Bessent and

the markets have been pining for, raising its official rate by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%.
Since intervening in the foreign exchange markets, the yen has appreciated by nearly 5%
against the U.S. dollar, alleviating concerns of a disorderly unwind of the yen and giving
Bessent—thus far—an important victory. Defending the yen, of course, isn’t simply about
Japan—it’s about the U.S. and the need for foreign capital inflows.
Japan is one of America’s largest foreign creditors. A persistently weak yen creates

pressure on Tokyo to defend its currency, potentially by selling some of its enormous
holdings of foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys. That prospect didn’t sit well with the
Treasury secretary. At precisely the moment Washington needs foreign capital to finance
its enormous fiscal deficits, the last thing Mr. Bessent wants is one of America’s largest
creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities. Hence, the battle to backstop

Japan’s currency.
Front Two: Defending the 10-Year Bond. Mr. Bessent has had less success on the
second front: the U.S. bond market. While the 10-year Treasury yield slipped slightly
following last week’s decision by the Fed to boost the fed funds rate by 25 bps, the
benchmark interest rate is up roughly 85 bps since the start of the year. Stubbornly high
inflation, elevated oil prices, enormous government borrowing requirements and growing
investor anxiety over America’s fiscal trajectory have all converged and conspired to keep

the 10-year yield closer to 5% than 4%.
Field Marshal Bessent is very aware that the 10-year Treasury is the gravitational center of
global finance—and that mortgage rates, corporate borrowing costs, equity valuations and
the government’s own interest bill all take their cue from it. A sustained move materially
above 5% would tighten financial conditions across the American economy, potentially
threaten the Equity bull market, and pile more pressure on the Republican Party.

To prevent this from happening, the Treasury has expanded its bond-buyback operations,
including purchases of longer-dated government securities, in an effort to improve liquidity
and relieve some pressure on the market, while shifting new government debt issuance
into short-term bills. Yet the bond vigilantes have so far proved harder to intimidate than
currency traders. Even after Treasury announced additional purchases, yields continued
climbing before receiving some relief from the latest hawkish move from the Fed.

Over the medium term, if investors believe Washington is borrowing too much, inflation is
running too hot or the supply of bonds is overwhelming global demand, they will demand a
higher yield to own America’s debt. This battle is far from over.
Front Three: Squeezing Tehran. Then there is Iran, where Mr. Bessent is wielding an
entirely different weapon: America’s control over the plumbing of global finance. In practice
Investment Implications
We expect continued investor

support and preference for real hard
assets, and a bias towards a plethora
of commodities and industrial plays.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
this means the Treasury can make a foreign bank choose between doing business
with Iran or the U.S. It can freeze assets, blacklist companies, restrict transactions and
pressure allies to close financial escape routes.
Under “Operation Economic Outcast,” billed as economic “D-Day,” Treasury has
intensified sanctions against Iranian entities and financial networks, including recent

actions against Iran aviation and additional efforts aimed at banks and organizations
facilitating Tehran’s international transactions. Mr. Bessent has described the objective
in unusually stark terms: to “economically asphyxiate” the Iran regime. This is economic
warfare in its purest form, but whether it works remains to be seen.
“Asphyxiating” an economy takes time and patience—and international cooperation
and coordination. It means closing multiple global loopholes of third-party entities still

willing and able to do business with Iran—look no further than Russian banks. In addition,
squeezing a major oil producer runs the risk of aggravating global oil supplies, keeping
oil prices elevated. Higher energy costs, in turn, feed right back into the U.S. inflation
outlook the Fed is trying to tame and keeps the bond vigilantes on guard and ready to
push back. Not unlike the battle to suppress U.S. bond yields, the battle to bring Iran to
heel is going to be a long one.

How does it all end? How Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A
decisive victory on all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets
is next to impossible, while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best-laid plans.
That said, however, the U.S. Treasury Secretary has signaled that he is ready to take
a very proactive, pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less
encouraging news stems from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the

Treasury secretary.
Mr. Bessent can pressure Iran because access to the dollar-based financial system
remains extraordinarily valuable. He can intervene in foreign-exchange markets because
America’s financial resources give Washington enormous clout. And he can attempt to
influence the Treasury market because U.S. government securities remain the foundation
of the global financial system. But therein lies the contradiction.
The U.S. is simultaneously weaponizing the financial system abroad while becoming more

dependent on that same financial system at home. Washington wants the world to fear
losing access to dollars while also asking the world to lend it trillions of dollars. It wants to
use Treasury and dollar dominance as instruments of geopolitical coercion while running
fiscal deficits that require foreigners to continue believing deeply in those very assets.
The more Washington borrows, the more sensitive it becomes to bond-market discipline.
The more aggressively it weaponizes access to the dollar, the greater the incentive for

adversaries to seek alternatives. And the more the Treasury secretary intervenes directly
in markets, the more investors may begin wondering where economic policy ends and
market management begins.

For the moment, Mr. Bessent is fighting on three fronts. He wants a stronger yen, a
lower 10-year Treasury yield and an economically isolated Iran. Success on any one front
would be difficult. Achieving all three simultaneously will require considerable skill—and
perhaps a little luck.

MARKET VIEW
Beyond AI’s Resource Footprint, Consider its “Handprint”
Anna Potts, CFA, Vice President and Investment Strategist
Since the launch of ChatGPT, investors have celebrated AI’s potential while growing
more concerned about its resource footprint: Behind every AI-generated response is
a physical network of data centers, power plants, transmission lines, cooling systems,

semiconductor facilities and critical-mineral supply chains. No doubt, the AI revolution
has environmental costs. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power, water,
advanced hardware and raw materials could strain grids, raise emissions and intensify
pressure on local resources. Yet the sustainability conversation should extend beyond
AI’s footprint to consider whether its environmental benefits can outweigh its
resource demands.
AI’s footprint is large, growing and certainly not trivial. AI increases electricity demand,

adds large, concentrated loads to already constrained grids, and may slow the retirement
of polluting energy assets. U.S. data centers could account for 20% of national electricity
consumption by 2035, up from 5.9% today, with power needs reaching 194 gigawatts
(comparable to the electricity demand of more than 150 million homes). 1 Cooling, power
generation and semiconductor manufacturing require water; each new megawatt also
embeds substantial copper, aluminum and other materials; and short hardware cycles

can increase electronic waste. Use varies sharply by workload: Complex reasoning and
agentic tasks can consume many times more energy than a simple text prompt. The
effects are already reaching consumers and communities. Wholesale power costs on
PJM—the largest U.S. grid—rose 76% year-over-year in Q1 2026, while data-center
load included in its previous two capacity auctions added an estimated $13.8 billion to
customer bills. 2 Communities are mobilizing around their concerns, including affordability,

water use and air pollution: Q1 2026 produced the largest single quarterly concentration
of delayed or blocked data-center projects on record. 3 Opposition is therefore becoming
a development constraint to be considered.
Context, caveats and comparisons. Headlines often tell only part of the story. Globally,
data centers account for about 1% of electricity use, 4 modest relative to many industries,
and 9% of estimated global 2025 to 2030 electricity growth. 5 A typical text prompt on

one major company’s AI model 6 uses 0.24 watt-hours of electricity, emits 0.03 grams
of CO₂ and consumes 0.26 milliliters of water—roughly 617,000 prompts match the
emissions from producing and cooking one sirloin steak, while 262,000 match the water
used in an eight-minute shower. 7 Still, billions of interactions, more complex workloads
and growth in resource-constrained regions can create meaningful cumulative impacts.
The most meaningful assessment therefore depends on workload, utilization, location,

grid mix, cooling design, local water availability and who bears the cost of supporting
infrastructure, not one universal number.
AI’s Handprint. While much remains to be seen, AI’s environmental “handprint”—the
benefits it can enable—could potentially prove larger than its direct footprint in some
areas. Across the energy system, AI can help utilities better match electricity supply
and demand, balance grids, optimize storage and reduce wasted energy. Data centers

already account for more than 30% of corporate renewable power-purchase agreements,
helping create long-dated demand for clean generation. 8 Beyond power, AI can improve
precision agriculture, streamline transportation and inventories, and support product
1 BloombergNEF , Data Centers on Track to Suck Up a Fifth of US Power Use by 2035, July 21, 2026.
2 BloombergNEF , AI Buildout Drives 76% Power Bill Jump on Largest US Grid, May 14, 2026.

Investment Implications
Responsible AI growth requires
investment in power generation,

grid modernization, energy storage,
advanced cooling, water treatment
and reuse, efficient computing, and
materials recovery and recycling.
Diversified environmental and thematic
strategies may offer exposure to
these physical enablers while reducing
reliance on benchmark names.

design, predictive maintenance, waste sorting and recycling. AI is transforming climate
risk management by using satellite imagery, weather data and predictive analytics to
forecast floods, wildfires and extreme weather events, enabling governments, companies,
and investors to anticipate risks and allocate capital more effectively (Exhibit 2). These
gains are not automatic: they depend on innovation, adoption, and whether efficiency

improvements reduce total resource use rather than simply stimulate more demand. All
industries are faced with the opportunity to responsibly use AI to make operations more
efficient, and much is yet to be discovered.

AI’s potential handprint becomes more tangible in transportation, agriculture and climate
resilience. In aviation, for example, AI can combine weather forecasts, flight paths and
atmospheric conditions to reroute aircraft around regions where warming contrails
are most likely to form—reducing climate impact without requiring a new aircraft or

fuel system. On farms, machine learning can interpret aerial imagery and field-level
sensor data to identify crop stress early, directing water, fertilizer and chemicals to
where they are needed and helping growers improve yields while reducing inputs. AI
can also strengthen climate preparedness by analyzing satellite imagery, weather data
and infrastructure exposure to detect deforestation, anticipate floods or wildfires,
and identify communities, facilities and supply chains most vulnerable to physical

hazards. These are just a few examples that illustrate how AI’s value can extend beyond
incremental efficiency gains to reshape real-world decisions.
Investment Considerations. The challenges created by AI also create potential
investment opportunities. Companies are investing in efficient chips, closed-loop cooling,
reclaimed-water systems, energy storage, grid upgrades and cleaner firm power to
reduce resource intensity and improve reliability. Professionally managed environmental

and thematic strategies can target these “picks and shovels” of the digital buildout,
alongside businesses using AI to improve efficiency across energy management,
water infrastructure, environmental services, waste management and compute. A
disciplined thematic approach can diversify exposure beyond the largest benchmark
constituents while positioning for structural growth in power demand, grid modernization
and resource efficiency. Focusing solely on AI’s footprint risks overlooking both the

companies helping it scale more efficiently and the broader environmental benefits its
handprint may enable.

THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
The Beauty of September
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Call it an eventful first 13 trading days of September: Brent crude re-eclipsed $100 per
barrel, diesel prices surpassed $6 per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S.
Treasury yield breached 5%, 9 and the Fed joined the ECB and the BoJ in hiking rates for

the first time since July 2023. Through it all, the S&P 500 has shed 1.4% since the start
of the month, while its equal-weighted counterpart is down by 3.3%.
It’s not all too unexpected that September would be a choppy month for U.S. Equities.
Historical seasonal patterns suggest as much. Since 1928, the average S&P 500 return
in September has come out to -1.1%, 10 not only the worst month of the year on average
but also the only month when the benchmark has ended lower more often than it’s

ended higher. Similar weakness has been seen in midterm election years, with the S&P
500 producing an average September return of -0.8% since 1950. 10
The good news is that this has historically been an attractive entry point for U.S. Equity
investors. Call it the beauty of September: In contrast to September’s average decline,
average returns improve in October (+3.0%), November (+2.7%), and December (+0.8%).
Indeed, the S&P 500 has never been negative in the six or 12 months post-midterm

election since 1950 (Exhibit 4A), boasting an impressive average return of 13.8% and
15.8% over those respective periods. 10
Looking ahead to the coming weeks, we have little reason to believe that 2026
will diverge from the historical trend. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election, in line with what’s typical in the months surrounding the
midterms (Exhibit 4B). A quieter corporate calendar ahead of Q3 earnings means

investor focus will remain on incoming macro data, the path of energy prices, and the
Fed’s policy path.
In the meantime, while the S&P 500 still stands just 2.7% below its mid-August high,
churn beneath the surface already appears to be taking place. Just 38% of S&P 500
members now stand above their 50-day moving average versus 70% a mere month ago. 9
Far from worrisome, a correction in the near term in the context of a strong fundamental
economic backdrop would be a potential buying opportunity in our view.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

### Page 2

ESG与可持续发展周报
3 方面考虑。一是能源革命是新一轮科技革命和产业变革的关键赛
道。当前，以绿色低碳为标志的能源革命正在全面跨过技术积累拐
点，进入到快速商业化规模化发展阶段。国际可再生能源署数据显
示，2010—2025 年，光伏、陆上风电度电成本分别累计下降 89%、
71%；2025 年全球超九成新建可再生能源发电项目成本低于化石能源
电站。国际能源署预计，2026 年全球能源总投资将达到 3.4 万亿美
元，其中 2.2 万亿美元投向风电、光伏、储能、氢能等清洁能源领
域，已成为全球布局新一轮科技革命和产业变革的重要方向。二是积
极推进能源革命是实现碳达峰碳中和的必由之路。从排放源头看，能
源活动贡献了我国 80%以上的碳排放量，其中电力行业碳排放超过全
社会碳排放的 40%，实现碳达峰碳中和目标的主要矛盾集中在以化石
能源为主的能源结构上。中国式现代化以实体经济为根基，能源的饭
碗必须端在自己手里。中长期看，我国能源刚性需求仍将继续增长。
从源头上减碳是实现碳达峰碳中和的治本之策，要求我们必须通过清

洁能源规模化扩张，覆盖新增用能需求，减少化石能源消费存量。三
是发展新质生产力需要通过能源革命来提供可靠安全保障。当前，地
区冲突频发加剧地缘政治风险，能源问题政治化、武器化属性更加突
出，全球能源市场波动加剧。发展新质生产力，建设现代化产业体
系，特别是满足全国一体化算力网、新一代通信网等新型基础设施建
设，以及集成电路、人工智能、新能源汽车等新兴产业用能增长需
求，推动产业高端化、智能化、绿色化，要求我们积极推动能源革
命，持续提高国内能源供给占比。
《意见》围绕着力推进发展方式创新，部署构建绿色低碳循环发
展的经济体系、优化绿色发展政策工具箱和积极推进能源革命三项举
措。我们认为，新质生产力推动可持续发展，我国正迈入以新质生产
力为核心驱动的绿色发展新阶段。
 行业动态
（1）近年来，我国保险业在面对重大自然灾害时，响应速度与理赔力
度不断提升，社会保障功能持续显现。今年 5月以来，受强降雨、台
风等影响，广西、湖北、甘肃等地接连发生城市内涝、水库溃坝、山
体滑坡等灾情。中国保险行业协会数据显示，截至 2026年 7月末，面

对 20 个省份的暴雨、台风等灾害，保险业已快速赔付 55.3 亿元。
2025 年全年，保险业针对重大自然灾害的赔付高达约 346.72 亿元。
当前我国保险赔付占自然灾害直接经济损失的比重仅约 10%，仍有较
大保障缺口。巨灾往往具有低频高损、破坏力极强的特点，仅靠事后
被动补偿，社会整体损失依然沉重。因此，破局的关键在于前置——
推动保险服务从出险以后赔向灾害来临前防延伸。前置风险防线，重
点在科技预警和风险减量。保险机构应把服务链条前移，建立覆盖多
灾种的监测预警和应急响应机制，运用气象监测、遥感、地理信息、
物联网等技术，对重点区域、重点标的进行动态跟踪，在灾害来临前
向客户精准预警，协助做好人员转移、设施加固、农作物抢收等防护
工作。将防灾减损费用纳入产品设计，与应急管理、气象、水利等部
门建立信息共享和联动机制，推动灾前预防、灾中干预、灾后理赔有
效衔接，真正降低灾害损失。
（2）10月 14日，全国首批绿证跨境集中交易（香港）发布活动在广
州举行，本次交易合计成交约 10万个国家可再生能源绿色电力证书，

对应可再生能源电量近 1亿千瓦时。本次交易实现内地绿证规模化进
入香港市场，搭建起内地绿证市场对接香港国际绿色金融市场的流通
渠道，是粤港澳大湾区推进绿色能源协同创新的标志性实践。

### Page 3

ESG与可持续发展周报
 技术前沿
10 月 15 日，中国石油塔里木 120 万吨乙烯工程商业运营新闻发
布会在北京举行，项目即日起全面投入商业运营。标志着中国石油在
新疆形成 300万吨级乙烯产业长周期稳定运营能力。作为我国首个全
链条绿色低碳乙烯工程、中西部单体规模最大乙烯项目，工程总投资
256.56 亿元，整体国产化率 99%。可年产 90 万吨全密度聚乙烯、45
万吨聚丙烯、10万吨顺丁橡胶，橡塑产品总产能达 145万吨，填补南
疆高端聚烯烃、合成橡胶新材料产业空白，带动区域下游形成千亿级
产业集群。
自今年 7月 16 日一次开车成功以来，工程已累计生产乙烯超 26
万吨，外供优质化工品 56.6 万吨，各类产品合格率达 100%，聚丙
烯、MTBE（甲基叔丁基醚）产品远销中亚市场。除此之外，工程搭
建“绿电直连+碳捕集+蓝氢+蓝氨+绿色化肥”全链条低碳技术闭环，
建成国内首套自主技术乙烯三机电驱装置和全球首个乙烯裂解炉烟气
碳循环利用示范基地。通过回收乙烯裂解炉烟气中的二氧化碳生产尿

素，实现“以废制肥”。目前，工程已捕集利用二氧化碳超 10万吨，
产出绿色尿素超 12万吨。工程可年减碳 137万吨，固碳生态效益相当
于新增 548万亩森林碳汇。
 研究探索
近日，由中国经济信息社主办的“蓄势与新篇：2026耐心资本与
可持续发展大会”在湖南长沙举行。在大会上，中国金融学会绿色金
融专业委员会主任、北京绿色金融与可持续发展研究院院长马骏表
示，我国逐步形成了以界定标准、信息披露、激励机制、产品体系为
四大支柱的绿色金融体系，绿色金融规模持续扩大。马骏指出，钢
铁、水泥、有色、化工等高碳行业低碳转型的融资需求，可能超过光
伏、风电等纯绿活动。应借助绿色科技提升转型项目的经济效益，识
别兼具减碳效益和经济性的技术路径。由其牵头筹建、获香港特区政
府支持的“绿色加速器”公益平台已经启动，将支持我国绿色科技企
业在“一带一路”沿线落地可融资项目，助力绿色企业出海。中国能
源研究会能源金融与法律分会主任委员陈景东在主旨演讲“绿色金
融——政策与实践”中表示，绿色财税和绿色金融为能源绿色低碳转

型发挥了重要作用。随着新能源行业不断发展，财税政策正在逐步调
整完善，绿色金融的支持政策和实践将更多来自资本市场。在绿色转
型过程中，能源企业需要更多权益类资金，希望财税和金融部门继续
加大对能源行业转型的支持。
 国际合作
10 月 20-22 日，“2026 可持续全球领导者大会”将在黄浦滨江
拉开帷幕。大会将汇聚来自全球的 300余位顶级嘉宾，包括联合国相
关机构、多个国际组织的代表、诺贝尔奖与图灵奖得主、国内外领军
企业高管及知名智库专家，围绕全球可持续发展的现实挑战与机遇开
展高水平对话。本届大会共有 22 个分论坛，议题涵盖：AI 发展与人
性未来，气候治理与生态保护，清洁能源与安全保障，绿色消费与品
牌使命，教育转型与科技赋能等。
 投资建议
《关于发展新质生产力的意见》围绕着力推进发展方式创新，部
署构建绿色低碳循环发展的经济体系、优化绿色发展政策工具箱和积
极推进能源革命三项举措。我们认为，新质生产力推动可持续发展，

### Page 1

14 October 2026
The bank
for a changing

EUROZONE: IMPROVING BUSINESS SENTIMENT, DRIVEN BY TECH, IS SPREADING TO OTHER SECTORS
Stéphane Colliac & Tarik Rharrab
*LATEST OBSERVATION: 5 OCTOBER 2026  **ESTIMATE
The September PMI surveys confirm that growth in the Eurozone is getting stronger, boosted in particular by new export orders, which are
still rising. Sectors that were already performing well – technology and capital goods (including for defence) – are continuing to grow, while

those that had been struggling until now – aeronautics, consumer goods and financial services – are seeing their production indices recover.
This strong performance is particularly noteworthy, given that it has not yet been undermined by higher inflation.
The heatmap shows the PMI index readings: a reading above 50 indicates expansion, while a reading below 50 signals contraction compared to the previous
month. For each indicator, sectors are ranked in descending order based on their September 2026 reading.

Shades of red represent values above 50, while shades of blue represent values below 50: the intensity of the colour increases the further away they are
from this threshold.
Production growth spreads to more sectors
The PMI surveys on business sentiment confirm that business activity in the Eurozone is expanding at a faster pace and spreading to more sectors.
Household-consumption-related sectors (goods and services), which were adversely affected in the spring by the outbreak of the conflict in the Middle

East, do not appear to be negatively impacted by the rising inflation observed since August. The aeronautics sector, where production has mainly been
limited by supply constraints, is also showing signs of improvement.
SOURCE: S&P GLOBAL, BNP PARIBAS

Chart of the Week / 14 October 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
These trends, although still moderate, come alongside a drive to upgrade equipment, fuelled by investment in digital technology, electrification
(which is supporting the automotive sector) and defence. The momentum in technology goods and services further strengthened in September.
The production index for computer and electronics hit a five-year high, while the software services production index stayed close to its July peak.

Financial services are also enjoying a significant improvement, reflecting the healthy lending momentum despite interest rates starting to rise
(see our analysis).
Growth driven by exports
Exports drove growth in the first half of 2026, both across the Eurozone as a whole (see our analysis) and among its key members (notably
Germany). This momentum is also increasing, as indicated by the ‘new export orders’ component of the various PMI indices. As was the case with

production, there has been a particularly noteworthy increase for capital goods (technology and machinery) and related services, particularly digital
services. This trend is playing out more broadly, as, in September, new export orders rose across all sub-sectors, with the exception of intermediate
goods and consumer goods, where contraction recently eased. There has been a significant improvement in the transport sector, particularly in
the aeronautics industry, which has major markets in Asia. Recent data on French exports show that aeronautics exports to this region are rising

once again.
The convergence between rising production and increasing export-order backlogs shows that Eurozone growth is based on an increasingly solid
foundation. Inflationary pressures are not holding it back for the time being.

Document 277: First Impressions_ NZ household incomes, assets and savings update - June quarter 2026 (2026-10-26)

Source PDF: all report pdf/First Impressions_ NZ household incomes, assets and savings update - June quarter 2026 (2026-10-26).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-26
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

#consumer #newzealand
First Impressions: NZ household incomes, assets and savings
update - June quarter 2026
By Satish Ranchhod
Senior Economist, Westpac NZ
10:24 October 08 2026 Share
New Zealand incomes and savings are rising, as is the value of households’ financial
assets. However, the value of housing assets remains flat.
Key takeouts from Stats NZ’s latest update on household incomes, assets and savings (June quarter
2026)

### Page 2

Household incomes
Disposable income levels in the economy grew by 5.2% in the year to June, down from 6.5% in the
year to March

Savings and wealth
Household savings have continued to rise, climbing by $2.5b over the past quarter.

Overall household wealth levels were up 1.9% over the past year. Under the surface, the make-up of
household wealth continues to shift.

Interest costs 
Overall household income levels are continuing to rise. Much of that is due to increases in
entrepreneurial earnings (which is in part due to increased earnings in the agricultural sector). Wage
and salary growth has been more limited but has picked up.
Household savings levels have been rising for three years now, with another solid rise over the past
three months. 
Overall household wealth levels have risen modestly. Although the value of households’ financial
assets is continuing to rise, housing assets have essentially shown no growth since late 2023.
Household spending on interest costs rose just slightly in the June quarter. That’s the first increase in
two years. The easing in average borrowing costs seen over the past few years has come to a close
with the RBNZ’s hiking cycle now in train.
Underpinning the rise in overall income levels have been continued increases in entrepreneurial
earnings, which are up 12% over the past year. That will include stronger earnings in the agricultural
sector, which has been boosted by the strength in commodity export prices. That lift in agricultural
earnings has been reflected in stronger economic confidence and spending in many rural regions.
We expect commodity export earnings will remain firm over the year ahead. 
In contrast, wage and salary earnings are up just 2.7% over the past year. That’s up from 1.8% in the
year to March, but it’s a fairly muted rise, especially given the continued increases in the population
and inflation of 4.1%.
June’s large increase in savings builds on the solid increases seen over the past three years.
The savings rate in the June quarter was 3.7% of disposable incomes, down just slightly from last
quarter.
The value of households’ financial assets has continued to rise and is up 4.6% over the past year. 
However, the value of housing and land assets is down 0.9% over the past year, and it has remained
essentially unchanged since late 2023. We expect housing market conditions will remain soft over
the coming year, limiting increases in overall household wealth. 
Households’ spending on interest costs rose just slightly in the June quarter, climbing by 0.3%.
While that’s a relatively small change, it is the first increase that we’ve seen in two years.

### Page 3

Related articles

Media Contact:
Satish Ranchhod, Senior Economist
P: +64 9 336 5668
M: +64 21 710 852
Over the past few years, many borrowers saw their debt servicing costs fall as they rolled off earlier
high mortgage rates and onto lower ones. That helped to support household spending.
However, the easing in average borrowing cost has now ended. Recent months have seen mortgage
rates pushing higher as the RBNZ has begun to hike the Official Cash Rate, with further hikes
expected over the coming year. 
Looking ahead, we don’t expect a significant increase in average household borrowing costs.
However, we won’t see the same support for spending that we did over the past few years.

For most of the last decade, nominal GDP has been above 10yr yields, but that has reversed,
which is challenging from a debt sustainability if sustained – especially when debt/GDP is
pushing up against 120%. Recent inflation will slowly push up nominal GDP but the
relationship between the two will be important going forward.

Inflation is always a double-edged sword when it comes to debt. An unexpected burst of
inflation can help lower debt, as we saw right after the pandemic, but it becomes a challenge
when higher yields offset this. This is now the 3rd biggest inflation spike since the Euro Area’s
formation in 1999.

All that said, DB’s rates strategists now see French bonds as cheap relative to their
fundamental metrics. This chart looks at the residuals of France’s twin deficit score, which is a
weighted average of the historical current account balance (% of GDP) and budget balance (%
of GDP), with a higher weight assigned to more recent years.

Looking at a variety of macro variables, they now see France as the cheapest European
government bond relative to fundamentals. So things are challenging at the moment, and the
long-term charts look worrying, but much of the sell-off in recent days/weeks has been due to a
clearing-out of carry trades, implemented assuming a much lower vol period than we’ve seen.

Document 355: The Netherlands defies Europe’s tech trend thanks to ASML

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Netherlands defies Europe’s tech trend thanks to ASML.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-26
Pages: 27
Status: ok

### Page 1

National Productivity Board
2024 annual report
Labour productivity in the
market sector declined by 1.4%
in 2023.
Exporters are the most productive
firms in the Netherlands, but also
their suppliers perform above
average.
CPB - February 2025
Leon Bettendorf &
Michael Polder (Statistics Netherlands)

### Page 2

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 2 of 27 
Preface 
Productivity has shifted to the top of the political agenda in the last years. In view of the limitations to a 
further expansion of the labour supply, productivity growth is the principal way to support our future living 
standard. Unfortunately, the Netherlands, as all OECD countries, has been struggling with a declining trend in 
productivity growth during the last decades. We do not yet fully understand the causes of this global 
productivity slowdown.

CPB is part of a network of independent National Productivity Boar ds in the EU. The CPB has been a 
National Productivity Board since 2017. The main NPB tasks are monitoring and analysing productivity 
developments in the Netherlands. As in previous years, we summarise research on productivity issues in this 
Annual Report 2024.

We are preparing three publications this year. First, we participate in a multi-country project of the 
Productivity Institute (UK). The aim of this project is to identify and compare country -specific productivity 
trends and pro-productivity policies. Second, we will analyse the evidence on changing bus iness dynamics in 
the Netherlands and the consequences for firm-level productivity and innovation. Finally, we will provide the 
analysis underlying our projections of long run productivity.

Pieter Hasekamp 
Director CPB

### Page 3

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 3 of 27 
Samenvatting 
De arbeidsproductiviteit in de marktsector daalde in 2023 met 1,4%. Sinds 2000 is de productiviteit alleen 
in 2009 nog sterker gedaald. De uitfasering van de gaswinning sinds 2014 heeft de jaarlijkse productiviteits -
groei met gemiddeld 0,3%-punt gedrukt.

We begrijpen nog niet helemaal waarom de productiviteitsgroei is afgenomen. Zoals in alle OESO-landen 
heeft Nederland last van een vertraging van de productiviteitsgroei. In een literatuuronderzoek vinden we 
verschillende verklaringen, maar de bijdrage van elke oorzaak is moeilijk te bepalen vanwege de sterke 
verbanden tussen de oorzaken.

Het productiviteitsverschil tussen de 10% meest productieve bedrijven en minder productieve bedrijven 
nam in 2021 toe. We stelden in ons vorige rapport (CPB, 2024) vast dat de productiviteitskloof tussen de 
bedrijven van de top 10% en de bedrijven van de onderste 10% sterk is toegenomen  in 2020. Uit een update van 
deze analyse blijkt dat de kloof in 2021 groot bleef en niet te wijten was aan de bijzondere omstandigheden in 
2020. Productiviteitsgroei in de economie kan voortkomen uit productiviteitsgroei binnen bestaande 
bedrijven of door activiteiten te verschuiven naar productievere bedrijven. De bijdrage van de verschuiving 
daalde in de verwerkende industrie en de dienstensector in de periode 2011-2019. De bijdrage van de 
bedrijvendynamiek bleef stabiel in de handel, terwijl deze belangrijker werd voor overige industrieën (bouw, 
energie, water en afval).

Exporteurs en hun directe toeleveranciers vormen de meest productieve groep bedrijven in Nederland.  
Uit de literatuur weten we dat exporterende bedrijven productiever zijn dan niet -exporterende bedrijven. We 
laten met nieuwe netwerkdata een duidelijk patroon zien: exporteurs zijn groter en productiever dan hun 
toeleveranciers, die op hun beurt beter presteren dan hun eigen toeleveranciers. De productiviteit van 
exporteurs en hun toeleveranciers wordt versterkt via drie mechanismen: leere ffecten, selectie en 
schaalvoordelen. Beleid dat bedrijfsnetwerken versterkt, kan bijdragen aan productiviteitsgroei.

### Page 4

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 4 of 27 
Summary

Labour productivity in the market sector declined by 1.4% in 2023. Since 2000, only 2009 saw a stronger fall 
in  productivity.. Phasing out gas extraction since 2014 depressed annual productivity growth by 0.3%-point on 
average.

We do not fully understand why the productivity growth has decreased. As in all OECD countries, the 
Netherlands is suffering from a slowdown in productivity growth. A survey of the literature provides various 
explanations, but the contribution of each cause is difficult to determine due to the strong links between the 
causes.

The productivity difference between frontier firms and non -frontier firms was growing in 2021. We 
observed that the productivity gap between the top-10% firms and the bottom-10% firms strongly increased in 
2020. An update of this analysis shows that the gap remained large in 2021. Productivity growth can originate 
from growth within existing firms or by reallocating activities to more productive firms. The contribution of 
reallocation declined in manufacturing and services during the period 2011 -2019. The contribution of business 
dynamics remained stable in trade while it became more important for other industries (construction, energy, 
wate and waste).

Exporters and their direct suppliers form the most productive group of firms in the Netherlands. From 
previous research we know that exporting firms are more productive than non-exporting firms. From new 
network data, a clear pattern emerges: exporters are larger and more productive than their suppliers, who, in 
turn, outperform their own suppliers. Productivity of exporters and their suppliers benefit through three main 
channels: learning effects, selection and economies of scale. Policies that strengthen firm networks can 
contribute to fostering productivity growth.

### Page 5

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 5 of 27 
1 Introduction 
This Annual Report summarises research on productivity developments in the Netherlands. 1 It consists of four 
parts.

In the next section we summarize the unfavourable productivity developments in 2023. We show that phasing 
out gas extraction since 2014 contributed to the low productivity growth rates in the Netherlands compared to 
other European countries.

Various explanations are found in the literature why productivity growth is slowing down. No consensus is yet 
reached on the contribution of each explanation of the slowdown. We discuss the main studies in the third 
section.

The fourth section, provided by Michael Polder (Statistics Netherlands), updates and extends the evidence on 
firm-level productivity. He documents productivity differences between firms and shows that the contribution 
of continuing firms, new firms and closing firms to productivity growth differ across industries, as well as 
between time periods.

The last section exploits new data on the Dutch production networks. 2 It is well known that exporting firms are 
more productive than an average firm. This section discusses the impact on productivity of firms that are 
suppliers to exporting firms.

1 Our Annual Report of 2023 can be found at annual report 2023. The reports of the other NPBs are collected at this EC-website. 
2 This is a translation of Freeman et al. (2024).

### Page 6

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 6 of 27 
2 Recent productivity developments 
Labour productivity in the market sector in 2023 was 1.4% lower than in 2022. Labour productivity is 
defined as value added per hour worked. 3 Figure 2.1a shows that productivity only declined more (with -2.2%) 
during the great recession in 2009 (CBS, 2024). The decomposition in figure 2.1b shows that the negative 
labour productivity growth in 2023 was driven by negative contributions of TFP growth (-0.8%) and capital 
deepening (-0.8%).4 The number of working hours has increased, while the capital stock reduced (due to non -
ICT capital). The long term decline in capital stock per hour since the mid 2010 -s seems to continue at an even 
faster pace. TFP growth (a measure of the efficiency with which labour and capital are used in the economy) 
turned negative in 2023. TFP grew fast in 2021 and 2022, in part through recovery from the corona pandemic 
shock in 2020

Figure 2.1 Labour productivity in de market sector declined in 2023

Source: CBS (2024); 2022 & 2023 are preliminary.

Phasing out gas extraction depressed productivity growth, starting in 2014. The mining and quarrying 
industry is very capital intensive, resulting in a high labour productivity. Labour productivity in this industry 
grew on average with 0.7% before 2014, while it fell with almost 16% per year after 2014  due to the phasing out 
of gas extraction (De Vries et al., 2024). Figure 2.2 shows that the average labour productivity growth in the 
Netherlands was one of the lowest in Europe. However, whereas the average contribution of the mining 
industry was negligible in most of the countries, it was negative ( -0.3%-point) in the Netherlands (and +0.2% 
in the US). The productivity growth of the market sector excluding mining in the Netherlands was much closer 
to the average value in Europe. Next to mining, other industries that contributed negatively to productivity 
growth in 2023 are transport and storage (-0.5%-point) and manufacturing (-0.4%-point).

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 8 of 27 
3 Explaining the productivity 
slowdown: a survey 
We do not sufficiently understand current productivity developments, which makes insight into future 
developments uncertain. As in many other countries, the Netherlands is suffering from a slowdown in 
productivity growth. In the literature, various explanations are put forward as to why growth has decreased, 
but the contribution of each cause is difficult to determine due to the  strong links between the causes. In 
addition, it is uncertain whether the outlined trend developments are permanent or temporary. For example, 
will artificial intelligence really develop into a general purpose technology that will put an end to the weak 
productivity growth? And, if so, how long will that last?  
All OECD countries are experiencing slowdowns in productivity growth. Figure 3.1a shows the annual 
growth of labour productivity in the Netherlands in the total economy and in the market sector (this is 
excluding the government). GDP per hour worked grew on average around 2 percent around the turn of the 
century. This growth has fallen back to below 1 percent after 2009. Figure 3.1b shows the slowdown in other 
countries. The decline in GDP growth per hour worked started in all countries before the Great Recession 
(2008-2012). The Great Recession may therefore have exacerbated the slo wdown but cannot be the cause 
(Fernald et al., 2023).

The global slowdown in productivity growth suggests common causes but there is no consensus yet on 
which cause is the most important and not even on whether the slowdown will be temporary or long -
term. This slowdown puzzle contributes to the great uncertainty of projections in the near and distant future. 
We discuss the most important, global causes one by one, although they are strongly related.  5

Figure 3.1 Labour productivity growth is falling (value added per hour worked)

Source: De Vries et al. (2024), figure 3.1.; De Bondt et al. (2021), figure 3.1.1.

5 See the surveys of Moss et al. (2020), Akcigit et al. (2021) and Goldin et al. (2024). For a discussion of specific Dutch causes, such as the 
large share of self-employed people and low R&D investments, we refer to Roelandt et al. (2019) and de Bondt et al. (2021).

### Page 9

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 9 of 27 
3.1 The diminishing potential of innovative ideas 
Technology pessimists are skeptical about innovations that can still significantly increase productivity. 
Gordon (2016) argues that the number and breadth of innovations was exceptional in the period 1870 -1970 and 
that we have now returned to the normal, structural level. Bloom et al. (2020) argue that the productivity of 
researchers has fallen sharply because it is becoming increasingly difficult to find new ideas. They express the 
TFP growth as the product of the number of researchers and their productivi ty. They observe a strong increase 
in the effective number of researchers, but the problem is that productivity in research has fallen just as 
sharply. The best illustration is provided by Moore's Law. The number of transistors in an integrated circuit has  
doubled every two years, but this required an increasing input of resources.

Jones (2022) lists three reasons why growth in GDP per capita will slow down in the future. Jones analyses 
economic growth in the US with a model in which TFP growth is determined by a Research & Development 
(R&D) sector. The first reason for a slowdown in growth is that the three main determinants after 1953, apart 
from population growth, can hardly grow any further. The contribution of population growth to growth in the 
past is 20%, while 80% stems from a rising level of education, an improved allocation of production factors 
and a growing size of the R&D sector (as % of population). The problem is that the last three factors are 
naturally limited. The second reason is that a slowdown in employment growth in R&D is already observed. 
The third reason is that natural population growth (excl. migration) is already negative in many developed 
countries. In the long run, economic growth remains proportional to population growth.

Technology optimists, on the other hand, emphasise the future potential of innovation in ICT;  see for 
example Brynjolfsson and McAfee (2014). As with previous industrial revolutions, they expect that it will take 
time before the combination and diffusion of multiple new technologies will lead to a revival of productivity 
growth. Artificial Intelligence (AI) could become a new general purpose technology. AI is expected to have 
major effects on the economy, and on productivity in particular, because AI not on ly increases the productivity 
of the production of products but also the production of innovative ideas (R&D). Initial studies estimate that 
AI increases macroeconomic productivity by 1%-point per year for 10 years (see the discussion in Aldasoro et 
al., 2024). However, Acemoglu (2024) is skeptical about the extrapolation of early, simple applications and 
calculates that TFP growth increases by less than 0.1%-points per year. In addition to the potential of AI, Jones 
(2022) believes that growth can accelerate in the future because there is still sufficient room for expansion of 
the R&D sector at the global level.

Philippon (2022) disputes the existence of a structural break in technological progress. He criticizes the 
standard specification of the TFP growth rate. The literature assumes a constant TFP growth rate (i.e. 
exponential growth), while Philippon argues that the evidence is more in line with a constant absolute growth 
rate (additive growth). If absolute growth remains constant, the TFP growth rate will decline in the future. In 
the US, he finds a structural break in TFP growth around 1930 in the period 1890 -2019. Due to catch-up growth, 
TFP growth in the eurozone and Japan was higher than in the US until 1990. After 1990, absolute growth 
declined sharply and became smaller than in the US. Assuming additive growth, Philippon finds no evidence 
of a recent slowdown in technological progress.

3.2 Divergence between high- and low-productivity firms 
The OECD found that the difference in productivity between high -productivity firms and low-productivity 
firms has increased (Berlingieri et al. 2017a). Because knowledge about new production techniques and 
organizational forms is spread less quickly, low-productivity firms are less able to keep up with the

### Page 10

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 10 of 27 
productivity growth of high-productivity firms. The deterioration in knowledge diffusion m ight be related to 
the increasing importance of intangible capital (see section 3.6). The divergent productivity development may 
explain the slowdown in productivity growth.

In the Netherlands, the productivity difference between the most productive and median firms started to 
increase around 2015. De Bondt et al. (2021, Table 5.3.2) find that the difference in both labo ur productivity 
and TFP increased before 2005 during the period 2000-2015. The divergence in productivity was stronger in 
industry than in the service sector. Research in van Heuvelen et al. (2018) confirms that the productivity gap 
between the top 10% firms and the median firms has remained stable in the period 2006-2015. In the next 
section we show with a new dataset that the top-median gap increased by 0.7% annually from 2015 to 2019. 
The divergence hypothesis is not a good explanation since the slowdown in productivity growth started much 
earlier in the Netherlands.

3.3 Increase in market concentration 
According to this hypothesis, lower productivity growth is associated with the trend towards less 
competitive markets. Firms with more market power have fewer incentives to increase their productivity. The 
emergence of less competitive markets is related to the superstars hypothesis (or winner -takes-all).6 After 
superstars have acquired a dominant market position, they shift their activities from innovative investments to 
strategies to protect their excess profits. De Ridder (2024) argues that the increase in intangible capital explains 
these developments. Since intangible capital is characterized by a combination of low marginal costs and high 
fixed costs, this promotes the growth of large firms and a reduction in productivity growth. The increase in 
market power seems to be a problem mainly in the US. 7 De Loecker et al. (2020) estimate the markup of firms 
(defined as the ratio of price and marginal costs) as a measure of market power. They find that the average 
markup in the US nearly tripled from 1980 to 2016.

However, an increase in market concentration can also promote productivity growth.  When more 
productive firms gain market share at the expense of less productive firms, the reallocation contributes to 
aggregate productivity growth.8 In Europe, the increase in market concentration does not seem to be 
associated with more abuse of market power. Bighelli et al. (2023) estimate stable markups in 15 European 
countries. Van Heuvelen et al. (2021) do not find that markups in the Netherlands  increased significantly in the 
period 2006-2016. The slowdown in productivity growth in the Netherlands therefore does not seem to be 
explained by the increase in the market power of firms.

3.4 Weakening of business dynamism 
Productivity growth has been affected by reduced business dynamism. With healthy business dynamism, 
old, low-productivity firms are replaced by more productive new firms (principle of creative destruction). 
Released inputs are put to more productive use, which contributes to a stimulation of productivity growth. 
Decker et al. (2017) point to the relationship between reduced business dynamism and slowed productivity 
growth in the US. Freeman et al. (2021) show that business dynamism in the Netherlands is slo wing down 
(after 2006), mainly as a result of a declining share of start-ups. They find that the reduced dynamism has

6 See e.g. Autor et al. (2020). 
7 See e.g. Eeckhout (2022). This hypothesis is contested by Shapiro et al. (2024). 
8 See the discussion of the distinction between good and bad concentration in Covarrubias et al. (2019).

### Page 11

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 11 of 27 
contributed to lower TFP growth (by 0.2%-points per year), especially in the service sector. The weakening of 
business dynamism may be a cause, but not the main cause of the slowdown in productivity growth.

3.5 Shifts in sector structure 
The shift towards the service sector reduces average productivity growth. The share of the service sector 
(incl. healthcare) in the economy increases, while the productivity of the service sector grows less than that of 
industry. De Bondt et al. (2021, section 5.2) confirm that the shift towards the healthcare and service sectors 
has had a negative effect on productivity growth in the period 1996 -2016, but that this shift is not large enough 
to really explain the slowdown in productivity growth (see an update in De Vries et al., 2024). Most of the 
productivity growth is found within the industries, while the negative reallocation effect between sectors 
remains small. Duernecker et al. (2023) compare the strong growth in labo ur productivity in the period 1970-
1985 with the slow growth in 2002-2017 for several high-income countries. They also show that the decline in 
productivity growth in the Netherlands (2.7%-points) is only partly due to the changed sector structure (0.5%-
points).

3.6 Measurement problems 
The literature also suggests that the growth slowdown is not a real phenomenon but is a consequence of 
larger measurement problems of the new economy. Measuring productivity faces classic problems, e.g. the 
correction for quality improvements in the healthcare sector. But the problems have been exacerbated by the 
digitalization of the economy.9 For example, before we could take digital photos, the productivity of 
developing and printing photos could be calculated. Nowadays, we store and view photos on our phones. How 
should the productivity of this ‘free’ service be calculated? However, calculat ions by Syverson (2021) indicate 
that measurement problems are probably insufficient to explain the productivity slowdown.

Measurement problems are specifically mentioned in the valuation of the increasing intangible capital  
stock. Intangibles consist of, for example, R&D, software, human and organizational capital. According to 
Brynjolfsson et al. (2021), errors in measuring productivity effects of investments in intangibles can be 
described with a J-curve. At the beginning of the project, productivity is underestimated because inputs are 
also used for the production of intangibles that are poorly measured. In the course of th e project, productivity 
is overestimated because intangibles are also used for the production of measured tangibles. In the 
Netherlands, too, intangible investments now exceed tangible investments, making it more difficult to 
estimate TFP growth.

3.7 Concluding remarks 
We conclude that the literature is still very uncertain about the true causes of the slowdown in 
productivity growth and about the development of trends in the future. It is still unclear what the 
contribution of each cause is to explaining the slowdown (see the approach in Goldin et al., 2024). And what is 
the role of links between the causes? The growing importance of intangibles seems to be related to the 
increasing market power of superstars and the reduced business dynamics. A second question is how 
permanent are the outlined trend developments? Will the technology pessimists or the optimists be right 
about the breakthrough of new, substantial innovations? Is the weakening of business dynamics a temporary

9 See the recent overview by Martin et al. (2024) and also De Bondt et al. (2021, section 6).

### Page 12

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 12 of 27 
or a long-term phenomenon? Given these uncertainties, CPB, as the NPB, will continue to study the low 
productivity growth.

4 Productivity differences across 
Dutch firms are large and 
persistent 
Michael Polder (Statistics Netherlands)

We present an update and extension of evidence on the dynamics of firm-level heterogeneity in labour 
productivity, and the relation between business dynamics and aggregate (industry -level) productivity. 
Understanding more about heterogeneity and the role of dynamics is essential for understanding aggregate 
growth. In particular, statistics about the within-industry dispersion in productivity are informative about 
whether the performance of firms in a particular industry is similar, or whether there are “win ners” and 
“losers”. A stylized fact in the literature is that the latter is the case (e.g. Syverson, 2011): the degree of 
heterogeneity in productivity is persuasive, even within narrowly defined industries . This is indicative for firm -
level differences in things such as production technologies and innovation, as well as market frictions 
(Bartelsman and Wolf, 2018). In addition, productivity differences are found to be closely linked to differences 
in renumeration of workers (Cunningham et al. 2023). Finally, macroeconomic growth depends in part on 
creative destruction, through which the least productive firms are driven out of the market, making way for 
new innovative and more productive firms, as well as reallocation of market shares towards more producti ve 
incumbent firms (Aghion et al. 2021).

A decade of productivity dispersion 
Frontier firms in the Netherlands are about twice as productive as the median firm, whereas the bottom 
group is about half as productive as the median. Productivity is measured as value added per hour worked. 
Dispersion is measured by differences between percentiles of the productivity distribution.  In particular, we 
express in figure 4.1 the labour productivity level of the top decile and the bottom decile relative to the median 
level. It shows large differences between firms at the top and the bottom of the distribution.

Note on data used 
We use an experimental dataset on firm-level productivity that is currently being developed at Statistics 
Netherlands. This dataset is an updated and improved version of that used in the previous edition of the 
National Productivity Monitor (CPB, 2024). The outcomes presented should be regarded as experimental. 
In addition, due to differences in delineation of the research population, methodological choices (e.g. 
outlier exclusion), and variable definitions, these figures may not fully align with official statistics. The 
dataset currently covers the period 2011-2021 and concerns incorporated enterprises only (legal forms “bv” 
and “nv”) in the business economy (excluding the financial and real estate sectors as well as petroleum). 
In the indicators we only consider firms with 3 persons (full-time) employed or more.

### Page 13

CPB PUBLICATIE – National Productivity Monitor Netherlands   Page 13 of 27 
Figure 4.1 Labour productivity, frontier (top-10%) and laggard (bottom-10%) firms compared to median, business 
economy, 2011-2021

Note: top, median and bottom refer to respectively 90th percentile, median, and 10th percentile of the productivity distribution. 
Productivity is measured as real value added over persons employed in full-time equivalents. Percentile deviations are calculated 
within-industry (NACE Rev. 2, two digit) and year. Only enterprises with 3 or more persons employed and legal personality 
(corporations) have been included, that are part of the Business Economy (NACE Rev. 2 B-N, excl. K, incl. 95; we also exclude 19 and L). 
Nominal value added is deflated using National Accounts industry-level deflators. For more on the underlying data, see CBS (2022a, 
Chapter 2).

Growing dispersion in the Netherlands can already be detected before the Covid period. Figure 4.2 shows 
the change in dispersion relative to the base year 2011. While the figure confirms the stable pattern found in 
van Heuvelen et al. (2018) for up to 2014/2015, an increase in dispersion can be detected for more recent years.  
This trend started already before Covid. From 2015 to 2019, the top-bottom gap increased by on average 1.2% 
annually, composed of a 0.7% increase of the top-median gap and a 0.5% between median and bottom. 
Growing dispersion has been documented globally (Andrew et al., 2016), but there is variation across countries 
(Berlingieri et al., 2017) and over time (e.g. in the case of US manufacturing, Cunningham et al., 2023).

The dispersion increased strongly in 2020 and more gradually in 2021. When the economy was hit by the 
Covid-shock for the first time, this resulted in a substantial decrease in macroeconomic productivity (see figure 
2.1).10 Nevertheless, figure 4.2 shows that this impact has been uneven across the business population, 
resulting in a productivity spread much higher than in previous years and notably a higher gap for the lowest 
part of the distribution. Moving further into the Covid period, the dispersion continued to increase. The top -
bottom gap grew in 2021 with 1.8%, mainly due to the frontier firms moving away from the median (1.2%).

10 We note that employment is in full-time equivalents and refers to hours worked according to contract.

Figure 4.2 Labour productivity dispersion, business economy, 2011-2021 (2011 = 1)

Multinational firms are strongly represented in the frontier group.  While the lion share of firms in the 
frontier group are independent small and medium enterprises (SMEs), their share is substantially lower than 
in the overall firm population (74.5% of frontier firms versus 83.6% in the population in 2021; table 4.1). 
Especially foreign multinational enterprises (MNEs) form a relatively large part of the frontier, compared to 
their population share (16.1% versus 9.3%). The post-2015 increase in dispersion has gone hand in hand with a 
slight shift within the frontier group, away from Dutch multinationals towards the other groups of firms.

We find that persistence at the frontier is substantial. The composition of the frontier group changes each 
year. The results in figure 4.3 suggest that persistence is rather high with over 80% of firms remaining at the 
frontier from year to year, or reappearing after a single year.11 This rate has been rather stable over time and 
across sectors.
